## 2026-11-18 05:55 UTC (Claude, data job): euro-area GDP (Eurostat's quarterly national accounts from the ECB Data Portal), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Chosen after listing, not built from memory.** The 02:4x note named euro GDP (`MNA`) with keys not tried. The first key guessed from memory (`Q.Y.I9.W2.S1.S1.B.B1GQ._Z._Z._Z.EUR.V.N`, the 20-country euro area) answered at once and looked right, but the latest GDP value of every reference area (32 series, 9 s) showed it ends at 2026-Q1: the dataflow keeps one series per composition of the euro area (`I6` and `I7` end 2015-Q4, `I8` 2024-Q1, `I9` 2026-Q1) and only `I10` (21 countries, as of 1 January 2026) is current, to 2026-Q3 for real GDP and 2026-Q2 for the breakdown. Then every seasonally adjusted series of `I10` (280, 7 s) and the whole record of the ten series the card uses (126 quarters from 1995-Q1, none missing) were read before any limit was fixed.
- **Source added.** The ECB Data Portal API (`data-api.ecb.europa.eu`, MNA dataflow, which is Eurostat's national accounts table 1 served through the ECB; free, no key): real GDP (chain-linked volumes) with its growth on the previous quarter and on the year, nominal GDP, domestic demand (`P3T5`), exports (`P6`) and imports (`P7`) at current prices and their contributions to growth, in four requests of 45 quarters. Checked live 2026-11-18 05:47 UTC: HTTP 200 for the four requests in 1.0 s, 1.9 s, 1.9 s and 1.8 s (1.9 s together, 0.9 s for the module's overview; the same four requests took 18.0 s and 0.8 s earlier in the hour).
- **What it shows (live, 2026-11-18).** Real GDP grew 0.41% on the previous quarter and 1.30% on the same quarter a year earlier in 2026-Q3 (status A); among the 40 quarters of the past ten years 48% had lower growth (the median 0.44%, the lowest -11.15% in 2020-Q2, the highest +11.54% in 2020-Q3); the last six quarters on the quarter: +0.41, +0.64, 0.00, +0.23, +0.32, +0.03. For 2026-Q2, the newest quarter with a breakdown: GDP at current prices €4,125.8 billion for the quarter (seasonally adjusted, not annualised), domestic demand €3,978.0 billion, exports €2,086.0 billion, imports €1,938.2 billion (net exports +€147.8 billion), and contributions to that quarter's real growth of 0.64%: domestic demand -0.27 points, exports +1.70, imports -0.79 (net trade +0.91).
- **What the checks are and are not.** Limits were set from the whole record before any test. Plain checks (properties, not the ECB's sums, said so on the card): units (the ECB's labels: levels EUR with multiplier 6, growth rates EUR with multiplier 1, contributions `EUR_R_B1GQ` with 0), the real series end on one quarter and the breakdown series on one quarter and the real ones are the same or the next, no quarter missing in the last 45, every figure inside a range set around the real one (real growth on the quarter -15 to 15 against a real -11.155 to +11.537, and the other nine in the data row of `DATA_SOURCES.md`), nominal GDP moving by at most 15% in a quarter (real -10.151% to +10.393%). **The source's own arithmetic, said so on the card:** (1) the growth rates equal the change of the real level, which is true by construction (the ECB calculates them from it: `DATA_COMP` reads `PCT(...)`; it proves the unit and the quarters' alignment, nothing else); (2) nominal GDP equals domestic demand plus exports minus imports (the real gap is at most 1.5e-8 millions of euro on all 126 quarters; tolerance 2 to the power -6); (3) the contributions of domestic demand, exports and imports add up to real GDP growth (at most 1.8e-15 points on all 121 quarters; tolerance 2 to the power -20; the contributions are the ECB's calculation too, so this is partly by construction). All three hold on the whole record without exception, so each covers the whole fetched window; they show the series belong to one release, not that the figures are right.
- **Found while building it.** (a) The stale predecessor above (loop-rules item 16, confirmed). (b) The ECB's `csvdata` has `DATA_COMP` and `COMPILING_ORG` columns: the growth rates and contributions are the ECB's (4F0) calculations from Eurostat's levels (4D0), so two of the three identities are partly or wholly by construction, and the card and the log say which (loop-rules item 14, extended). (c) The growth series are labelled with the unit of the level (EUR, multiplier 1) although they are percent; the growth-from-level identity proves they are. (d) Real GDP is a quarter ahead of the breakdown (about 45 against 65 days), so the card shows two quarters and says why. (e) Four quarters of the real exports and imports levels carry status E (estimated); not used. (f) Tolerances chosen exact in binary (2 to the power -6, -20 and -30) made a gap of exactly the tolerance testable (loop-rules item 15, extended). (g) The four requests took 0.8 s on one run and 18.0 s on another a few minutes earlier: the phone's timeout for this card is 60 s.
- **Found by the breakage run.** 321 deliberate one-line changes (every constant, every limit on both sides, every comparison and the order of the checks, the sums and signs of the three identities, the window, the rounding, the four requests, the cache times): 309 caught at first, 12 not. Eight were gaps in the tests, all closed: a quarter code with a two or three digit year passing the pattern; the number format of the limits (every limit is a whole number, so the other branch never ran); the year-on-year growth, the net trade and the exports contribution printed to one decimal where the real figures (1.2973, 147.8002 and 1.696) print the same at one and two decimals (a synthetic world with distinct decimals closed all three); an error handler that no real response reaches (a missing key, now forced); the status of the newest growth rate not carried from the response to the answer (the provisional tests skipped the transport); and the age counted to the machine's date instead of the date given, which survived only because the sandbox date equals the tests' date (a test that fixes `now` needs a second `now` that differs; loop-rules item 15, extended). Four are equivalent and cannot be killed: the real GDP's end quarter read from the level instead of the growth rate (the three real series must already end on one quarter); the quarters sorted by name instead of by number (the two orders agree for `YYYY-Qn`); and `<=` against `<` at exactly -15% and +15% on nominal GDP's one-quarter move (no pair of doubles gives exactly 15.0 for 100 times the ratio minus one: the neighbours land on 14.999999999999991 and 15.000000000000013). Second run of the twelve and three neighbours: 11 of 15 caught, the four left being those equivalent ones: **317 of 317 killable changes caught.**
- **Verified.** 32 new tests in `test_eurogdp.py` on a real fixture (the ten series for 45 quarters with the source's own digits, and the raw layout of a real response with its quoted commas): every figure worked by hand and in plain Python outside the module, every check on both sides of its limit, each series' units, the end quarters, the holes, the order of the checks, the reach of each check (the whole window, except the oldest quarters that have no earlier quarter to compare with), tolerances of exactly the tolerance in both signs, the real record never refused and its largest real gaps pinned, the four requests with the `+` left unencoded. Whole backend suite 2,451 passed; phone typecheck clean, 78 unit tests, `export:web`; browser tests 62 of 62 with every live card present (a first full run also gave 62 of 62, but the consumer prices card was briefly unavailable while the backend suite ran beside it, so it was repeated alone); the Macro accessibility probe measured 40 card source links (the smallest 45 points) with no violation; the top and the bottom of the card read in real screenshots; the module's live run gave the same figures as the fixture.
- **Not verified.** The ECB's and Eurostat's terms (the website hosts are refused here; the re-use policy is from memory); that the euro-area aggregate is a good picture of any one country (it is Eurostat's aggregation of the 21 members); a one-quarter move of nominal GDP beyond 15% would hide the card by design (the largest real move is 10.4%); that the next euro-area enlargement keeps `I10` current (the freshness mark, OVERDUE after 135 days, is what would notice it, as it should have for `I9`); the card on a real phone and with a real screen reader. 
- **Credibly profitable?** Not applicable to this job (a data card describes growth that already happened, not a signal). Status: not yet.
- **Next candidates.** ECB bank lending (`BSI` loans), the euro-area employment and labour productivity series of this same dataflow, or the sovereign-stress series of the CISS dataflow. 06 UTC improve: the breakage harness in the repo, or the repeated check wording on the Macro cards (both in `LOOP.md`).

- **The tool.** `python -m atlas_api.btcquant.crosscheck` (module `atlas_api/btcquant/crosscheck.py`, read-only: it opens the stored file for reading and writes no candle file, ledger, report or state; nothing calls it by itself). It asks Hyperliquid's public API for the daily candles of its Bitcoin contract (free, no key, one request), lines them up with the stored candles by UTC day and prints: the days in both files and the days in one only; the median, the 99th percentile (nearest rank) and the largest difference of the closes (venue against stored, in percent of the stored close); the days over 0.5% and over 2%; the daily returns' pairs, correlation and same-sign count; the newest common candle. **FLAG lines:** the newest common candle differs by more than 1%; any day differs by more than 2%; the correlation of the returns is under 0.99; closes that are not a positive number (left out, and said); no day in common. The limits are options (`--mid-pct --big-pct --warn-pct --min-correlation`, `--file`). A flag is printed, never an error (exit code 0); exit code 2 means the check could not be made, in words (the venue cannot be reached or answered HTTP 4xx/5xx, did not send a list, sent 5,000 candles or one that cannot be read; the stored file is missing, empty, has a wrong header or a damaged row or is a folder). Every report says that Hyperliquid's contract is a perpetual future, not spot, and that the check looks for a stored candle that is wrong, not for a tradable difference.
- **Live run, 07:43 UTC** (0.8 s, exit code 0, no flags): 2,282 days in both files, 2020-08-19 to 2026-11-17 (596 only in the stored file, 0 only at the venue); median 0.056%, 99% of days within 0.272%, largest -1.058% on 2023-03-12, 3 days over 0.5%, 0 over 2%; 2,281 return pairs, correlation 0.99976, same sign on 2,259; newest common candle 2026-11-17, stored 64873.18 against 64922.0 at the venue (+0.075%). These are the figures of the hand check at 01 UTC (`DATA_OPTIONS.md` section 5), which wrote the median as 0.055% (the median of the 2,282 sizes is 0.05551, which rounds to 0.056) and the days only in the stored file as 597 (2019-01-01 to 2020-08-18 is 596 days; both corrected there). The stored file's hash (`fb62274e1b52eeb5`) and `git status` of `btcquant` were the same before and after.
- **Proof it finds a wrong candle.** On a copy of the real file in the scratch folder, one close was made 5% too high (2024-06-10) and one was set to zero (2025-03-03): the report said "largest -4.653% on 2024-06-10; 4 days over 0.5%, 1 over 2%", "FLAG: 1 close(s) in the stored file are not a positive number (the first on 2025-03-03): they are left out" and "FLAG: 1 day(s) differ by more than 2%: look at them before trusting the file", and exited with 0. The real file was not touched.
- **Found while testing.** The first version would have stopped with a traceback on a stored file with a wrong header or a damaged row (the data module raises its own error there) and with a division by zero on a zero close, which is exactly the kind of candle the tool exists to report; both are said in words now (tests for the four kinds of unreadable file, a damaged compressed file and a folder, and for zero, negative, NaN and infinite closes on either side). The four limits now live in one table (`LIMITS`) that the function's defaults and the command line both read: two copies of the same four numbers could drift apart.
- **Verified.** Tests first: `tests/test_btcquant_crosscheck.py`, 53 tests, with a saved real answer of both venues (`tests/fixtures/hyperliquid_btc_1d.json`: the venue's last 60 daily candles and the 59 matching stored rows) whose figures were worked out in plain Python, apart from the tool (59 days, median 0.069%, 99% within 0.214%, none over 0.5%, correlation 0.99982, same sign on 57 of 58, newest +0.075%); hand-made worlds with limits that sit exactly on a value (+0.78125% is exact in binary) so `>` against `>=` is tested at the threshold; a 150-day world for the percentile; a second `now` far from the machine's date; the machine's time zone set to UTC-8 to prove the days are UTC days; the request body, the user agent, every failure of the venue, the client left open when given and closed when made (also when the venue fails), the whole summary word for word, and the module run as a command. **Breakage run: 205 deliberate changes** (constants and limits, the request, status and parsing, the closes, alignment by day, the sorted sizes and the percentile, every threshold and flag text, the client, the file errors, the summary and the command line): 7 survived the first run (the machine clock when no `now` is given; the first of several bad days; a flag for exactly one day over the big limit; the correlation limit written as `1.0` instead of `1`; the median of an even number of days; an empty `--file`; the summary's two one-sided counts swapped), each closed with a test line; second run 202 of 202 caught, and 3 ordering changes added afterwards (205 of 205). Changes that cannot be seen from outside are not in the table: `from None` against `from e` on a raised error, the argparse help texts, and `int(...)` on a JSON number. whole backend suite 2,506 passed.
- **Not verified.** That a perpetual future's close says more about Coinbase's than "no single bad candle in the overlap is large"; Hyperliquid's terms of use; the 596 days before 2020-08-19, the hourly file and the extra candles of 14f, which have no second source; the tool on the owner's machine.
- **Credibly profitable?** Not applicable (a data check; no strategy was tested). Status: not yet.
- **Next candidates.** A warning line in the daily report from this tool would change the daily run (one more request at 00:15 UTC and a new line in the report), so it waits for the owner (`LOOP.md`). 08 UTC data: ECB bank lending (`BSI` loans), euro employment and productivity (`MNA`) or CISS sovereign stress. 09 UTC skills: nothing new unless a gap shows. 10 UTC strategy: no change unless the owner chooses a way out of 14e.

## 2026-11-18 09:06 UTC (Claude, data job): euro-area bank lending (the ECB's loans of euro-area banks to firms and households, from the ECB Data Portal), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Chosen after listing, not built from memory.** The 05:4x note named three candidates (ECB bank lending `BSI`, euro employment and productivity, CISS sovereign stress). Bank lending pairs with the money supply card (the credit side of the same banks' balance sheet), so it was taken first. The euro-area monthly list of the whole BSI dataflow (`.../BSI/M.U2.........?lastNObservations=1&format=csvdata`: the key needs all 11 dimensions, so nine empty ones; 4,221 series, 15.9 s) showed that the ECB's headline measure is the ADJUSTED family `A20T` (loans adjusted for sales and securitisation), that the unadjusted `A20` and the purposes `A21`, `A22`, `A23` answer beside it without any error, and that every key is current to 2026-09 (4,169 of the 4,221). The key was then written from the listing.
- **Source added.** The ECB Data Portal API (`data-api.ecb.europa.eu`, BSI dataflow; free, no key): 24 series in four requests, the amounts outstanding, the monthly net flows and the annual growth of loans to firms (total, up to one year, one to five years, over five years) and to households (total, house purchase, consumer credit, other lending). The card sits on the Macro tab after the money supply card and shows the newest month with its place among the past ten years, the amounts and the month's net lending, the parts, and the last six months; the module is `atlas_api/eurolending.py`, the route `/api/macro/euro-lending`, the scheduler's macro refresh warms it, and it is OVERDUE after 75 days.
- **What it shows (live, 2026-11-18).** Loans to firms grew 4.24% over the twelve months to 2026-09 (2.90% a year earlier) and loans to households 3.07% (2.62%), both provisional; 72% (firms) and 58% (households) of the past ten years' months were slower. Euro-area banks had lent €5.52 trillion to firms (net new lending of €13.2 billion in the month) and €7.28 trillion to households (€15.1 billion). Households: house purchase €5.75 trillion (79.0%, +3.03%), consumer credit €0.85 trillion (11.7%, +4.84%), other lending €0.68 trillion (9.3%, +1.30%). Firms: up to one year €0.91 trillion (16.5%, +6.72%), one to five years €1.20 trillion (21.7%, +4.25%), over five years €3.41 trillion (61.9%, +3.61%).
- **What the checks are and are not.** Limits were set from the whole record (285 months of amounts for the two totals, the parts from 2014 and 2022) before any test: units from the ECB's own labels, one common last month, 36 months present in every series, ranges around the real extremes, no monthly change of an amount beyond -12.5% or +12.5% (real -4.26% to +3.39%), growth rates inside ranges (plain checks, said so on the card). **Two checks are the source's own arithmetic**: the totals equal their parts for the amounts and the flows (exact in every month of the common histories), and the published annual growth equals the growth rebuilt from the flows and amounts (largest gap 0.0235 points in 273 months, tolerance 0.0625, the last 24 months); the ECB builds them that way, so they show that the series belong together, not that the figures are right.
- **Found while building it.** (a) The record is shorter for the parts than for the totals (household purposes from 2014-12, firms' maturities from 2022-01), so the checks cover the shortest common history and the card says so. (b) The ECB portal answered the same four keys in 1.4, 1.4, 2.1 and 23.2 s one after the other and in 0.1 to 1.1 s together straight after, and the module's first call timed out at 30 s (the card went unavailable for its two-minute failure memory): the provider's timeout is now 45 s and the phone waits 60 s. (c) The month-on-month limit is -12.5% to +12.5% rather than a round 10% because 0.125 is exact in binary, so a change of exactly the limit can be tested. (d) Reading my own tests before the breakage run found placeholders in them that cannot fail (an `or True`, an `if False else`, a `[:0]`); the same patterns turned up in two older test files, where one assertion had never been made. Fixed, and `tests/test_test_hygiene.py` now refuses them everywhere (loop-rules item 18).
- **Found by the breakage run.** The first run made 348 deliberate changes (the endpoint, the entities and keys, the units, the requests, the limits and tolerances, reading the answer, every check and its words, what the card reports, the cache and the client): 341 were caught and 7 survived. Five were gaps in the tests, closed with tests: a monthly change in an old month of the window was never tried (the check could have been limited to the last 24 months), the share of lower months was only tested on 120 months (so dividing by the window constant instead of the months there are survived), the note's sentence that the newest month is provisional was not pinned, an answer other than 200 and 503 was never tried (an error status below 500 would have slipped through to the next error), and a cached answer's time was never compared with the clock of the later call. Two were equivalent and are not counted: `str(x)` instead of the `isinstance` test in `_month` (the text of a value that is not a string never matches the month pattern) and the tolerance written without `:g` (0.0625 prints the same either way). Second run: 346 of 346 changes caught (412 s, the module restored byte for byte, same hash). After the last wording fix (the word ADJUSTED in the note, lower case now) the five changes to the note were run again: 5 of 5 caught.
- **Verified.** 42 new tests in `test_eurolending.py` on a real fixture (the 24 series for up to 125 months, with the source's own digits, and the raw layout of a real response): the real card by hand and in plain Python outside the module (the sums, the rebuilt growth 4.244762 against the published 4.244776, the ten-year counts 86 and 70 of 120), every check on both sides of its limit (the exact edges at 12.5%, the million euro and 2 to the power -4, in worlds that pass every other check by construction), the order of the checks, every refusal by its words, the four requests expanded and compared with the 24 series, the cache, the timeout, demo mode and the route. whole backend suite 2,550 passed; phone typecheck clean, 78 unit tests, `export:web`; browser tests 62 of 62 with every live card present (the new card's content checks ran) and the healthy scenario 31 of 31 again after the last wording fix; the Macro accessibility probe measured 41 card source links (the smallest 45 points high) with no violation; the top and bottom of the new card read in screenshots (`07zf-eurolending-top`, `07zf-eurolending-bottom`).
- **Not verified.** The ECB's terms (the website hosts are refused here; the re-use policy is from memory); why the January 2022, January 2023 and January 2026 amounts jump (they coincide with the start of the maturity series and with new members of the euro area, which was not read in the source); that the growth of euro-area bank credit says anything about the next month; the card on a real phone.
- **Credibly profitable?** Not applicable to this job (a data card describes lending that already happened, not a signal). Status: not yet.
- **Next candidates.** The ECB's interest rates on new loans and deposits (the `MIR` dataflow: what firms and households pay), the euro-area employment and labour productivity series of the `MNA` dataflow, or the sovereign-stress series of the CISS dataflow. 09 UTC skills: nothing new unless a gap shows. 10 UTC strategy: no change unless the owner chooses a way out of 14e.

## 2026-11-18 10:49 UTC (Claude, strategy job; owner mission): no family run (the owner has not chosen a way out of 14e); the independent check of the paper accounts is now a tool
- **Checked.** Nobody else has pushed (origin is still `c9ef7df`); the draft pull request (#7) has no comments and no reviews; both triggers are enabled (daily next run 2026-11-19 00:15 UTC); of the modules the daily run imports, none has changed since the 04:19 rehearsal (only `view.py` at 06 UTC and `crosscheck.py` at 07 UTC changed in `btcquant`, and the run imports neither, nor the new `audit.py`). `btcquant/DATA_OPTIONS.md` stands, the three entries left in the research queue stay DEFERRED, and no family was pre-registered or run.
- **The one-off first.** A plain-Python script, written apart from the code (nothing from `atlas_api` imported), replayed the ledger of every paper account and compared it with the account file, the stored candles and the dashboard: all 18 accounts agreed. That answered the question once; the repository now has it as a tool, so any later hour can ask it again.
- **The tool.** `python -m atlas_api.btcquant.audit` (module `atlas_api/btcquant/audit.py`; `--root` names another btcquant folder). Read-only: it writes no candle file, account, ledger, report or state file, uses nothing of the engine (`account.py`, `paper.py`) and is called by nothing. Per account it checks: the files belong together (the account file and its meta carry the id that is the file's name; every ledger row names that account); the ledger begins with exactly one `account_opened`, its rows are numbered 1, 2, 3 ... and its start equity is the one in the account file and in the meta; every fill follows the rules (after an order: on the day after the signal bar, at that candle's open, the order's reference close being the close of its signal bar; a stop-out: no order, a `stop_hit` row, a low that reached the stop, filled at the stop or at the open when it gaps through it); the price is the reference moved 0.05% against the trade, the fee 0.10% of the notional and the slippage cost the quantity times the move; the cash and quantity after every fill equal the replay and are never negative (no short, no leverage); the file's totals (cash, quantity, fees, slippage, volume, number of fills) equal the replay; every curve day follows the day before, the first being the day after the candle the account opened after, with the stored close, the cash and quantity the ledger had that day, and the equity and exposure that follow; the last bar is the same in the curve, the account and the meta and no day behind the newest stored candle; a pending order is in the ledger and not filled yet; the dashboard shows the curve's last equity. The summary gives the counts and the largest difference of each kind; exit code 0 is no problem, 1 is problems (each in words, per account), 2 is that the audit could not be made (a missing or damaged file, in words). Its docstring says what it cannot say: that a strategy is any good, that a signal was the right one, that the candles are right.
- **Live run, 10:47 UTC** (0.04 s, exit code 0): 18 accounts, 5 fills and 46 curve days replayed against 2,878 stored candles (2019-01-01 to 2026-11-17); the largest differences between the replay and the files are all exactly 0 (cash, quantity, equity, price, fee: the replay does the engine's arithmetic in the engine's order); the 80 files under `btcquant` had the same hash before and after (`3f23ef7215874874`). The real ledgers hold 18 openings, 8 orders and 5 fills and nothing else yet (no `stop_hit`, `reject` or `breaker` row), so the stop-out rules have been tried on the hand-made worlds only.
- **Proof it finds damage.** On a copy of the real state in the scratch folder one fill's fee was made 1% too high, one curve day's equity 1 dollar too high and one dashboard equity 5 dollars too high: the audit printed 14 problems (the fee, the cash and the totals that follow from it, three curve days' cash, equity and exposure, the dashboard), largest differences cash 0.00809, equity 1.01, fee 0.00809, exit code 1. The real folder was not touched and still says "No problems."
- **Found while building.** The first replay added the signed notional and the fee in another order than the engine, which left rounding differences in the summary; it follows the engine's order now, so agreement is exact and the tolerance (1e-9, relative) is about real differences. A fill with no order or no candle used to be skipped, which hid it and set off 13 follow-on problems in one test; it is reported and still replayed now. **The breakage run's first table (254 changes) let 32 through:** 1 was equivalent (a candle that opens exactly at the stop: "the open" and "the stop" are the same number; left out of the table), 28 were gaps in the tests and are closed (the comparison's edge and its scaling with the larger number, the dates under a machine clock that is not UTC, candle files with a bad number or a short row, an account file that is not an object, an empty ledger and an opening that is not the first row, a low exactly at the stop, five tolerances, a small negative cash, the five "largest difference" figures, an account worth nothing, a curve that stops a day early, an account one day behind, and each kind of damaged layout), and 3 led to changes in the code: an account whose ledger cannot be replayed counted its stored curve days as replayed (it counts none now); `--root ""` quietly audited the default folder (an empty path is a path now, and fails in words, because a shell variable that was never set must not be audited as the repository); and an `IndexError` handler that nothing could reach was removed. Writing the layout tests showed one more: a ledger line that is JSON but not an object, or a pending order that is not an object, ended in a traceback (an `AttributeError` is a layout problem now). The audit also lacked two rules that a swapped or copied file would have slipped past (two accounts that hold the same would pass a replay): the ids and the ledger rows' account names, listed above; added with tests, and true of all 18 real accounts (31 ledger rows).
- **Verified.** Tests first: `tests/test_btcquant_audit.py`, 68 tests. A world worked out by hand (six candles and three accounts: one in cash, one that bought 90 Bitcoin and held, one that bought 50 and was stopped out at 95; the figures are checked in the first test outside the module: price 102.051, fee 9.18459, cash left 806.22541, the stop-out at 94.9525 with proceeds 4,747.625 and cash 9,635.224825), the same world with a gap through the stop (sold at the open of 90, price 89.955), and every rule broken one at a time; comparisons at exactly the edge with numbers that are exact in binary (15 against 16 at a sixteenth, a low exactly at the stop); the dates under a clock eight hours behind UTC; every kind of unreadable file in words; each kind of damaged layout as one problem for its account while the others are still audited; the audit proved read-only (not a byte, file or timestamp changed); the whole summary word for word; the exit codes; the module run as a command; and the repository's own state passing. **Breakage run: 254 changes in the first table, 222 caught; the second table (the first less the equivalent change, plus changes for the new code) 270 of 270.** Changes that cannot be seen from outside are not in the table (`from None` against `from e` on a raised error, argparse help texts). Whole backend suite 2,618 passed.
- **Not verified.** That the stored candles are right (`crosscheck.py` compares them with a second venue; neither check is proof); that the signals were the right signals (the audit takes every order as written) or that the protocol's fee and slippage are what a real venue would charge; stop-outs, rejections and the 15% circuit breaker on real ledgers (none exists yet); the tool on the owner's machine.
- **Credibly profitable?** Not applicable (a record check; no strategy was tested). Status: not yet.
- **Next candidates.** A line from this audit in the daily report would change the daily run (a new line in every report, one more way for it to fail), so it waits for the owner together with the second-source warning (`LOOP.md`). 11 UTC data: ECB interest rates on loans (`MIR`), euro employment and productivity (`MNA`) or CISS sovereign stress. 12 UTC improve: the breakage harness and the board tool (`LOOP.md`), the repeated check wording on the Macro cards. 13 UTC strategy: no change unless the owner chooses a way out of 14e.

## 2026-11-18 12:33 UTC (Claude, data job): euro-area bank interest rates (what banks charge on new loans and pay on new deposits, from the ECB Data Portal), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Source and why this one.** The 08 UTC note named the ECB's interest rates on new loans and deposits as the next candidate: the bank lending card shows how much banks lend, this one shows what borrowers pay and savers get. MIR dataflow of the ECB Data Portal (MFI interest rate statistics), euro area (`U2`, changing composition), monthly, new business: loans to firms, to households for house purchase, consumer credit and other purposes; agreed-maturity and overnight deposits of households and firms; the ECB's two composite cost-of-borrowing indicators (firms, house purchase). 40 series in four requests (`lastNObservations` 125), free, no key, the same host and client as the other ECB cards; checked live from this machine, HTTP 200 for all four (11.1, 11.2, 11.5 and 7.1 s one after the other). The ECB's re-use terms (free re-use with the source acknowledged) are from memory: its website is refused by the proxy.
- **Listing first, then the whole record, then the limits.** The whole euro-area slice of the dataflow in one request gave no answer in 120 s, so it was listed by counterpart sector and data type (101 rates for firms, 44 for households, 97 and 34 volumes, 5 to 13 s each, all current to 2026-09, newest month provisional except the two composites). The whole record of the 40 series chosen (four requests without a limit, 285 months from 2003-01, a few rates back to 2000 with the status "estimated", no month missing, no empty value) then gave the limits before any test was written: rates -0.36% to 8.91% (limit -1% to 12%), volumes 1,120 to 422,182 million euro (limit 100 to 1,000,000), largest one-month moves of the printed rates +0.59 and -0.72 points (limit 2) and of the overnight rates +0.10 and -0.37 (limit 1); the parts of a block are NOT held to the move limit because consumer credit with a floating rate moved +1.34 and -1.86 points in 2023 and 2020, so a real event must not turn the card off. Two identities hold in every month of every block (6 blocks x 285 months): the volume of all new business equals its two parts to 0.010 million euro (rounding of two decimals), and the rate on all of it equals the average of the two parts' rates weighted by their volumes to 0.0093 points (two roundings of 0.005 make at most 0.01); tolerances 2 to the power -6 (0.015625), exact in binary. The ECB's composites have no identity (they are weighted by a moving average of the volumes): bounded within one point of the rate on all new loans of the same borrowers (the whole record: -0.03 to +0.48 points for firms, -0.11 to +0.12 for house purchase), said on the card to be a loose bound. Sums and the plain checks look at the last 36 months, the rate identity at the last 24 (no failure anywhere in the record, so reaching further back would only widen the exposure to a future revision).
- **What the card shows** (`GET /api/macro/euro-bank-rates`, `atlas_api/eurobankrates.py`; Macro tab right after the bank lending card, `EuroBankRatesCard.tsx`): September 2026, provisional. Loans: firms 3.79% a year (+0.16 points on a month earlier, +0.41 on a year earlier; new loans €307.0 billion in the month), house purchase 3.62% (+0.02, +0.30; €75.8 billion), consumer credit 7.82% (-0.12, +0.42; €29.4 billion), other loans to households 4.38% (-0.01, +0.30; €13.4 billion). The ECB's cost of borrowing: firms 3.90% (+0.13, +0.40; 78% of the past 120 months lower; range 1.36% in December 2021 to 5.28% in October 2023), house purchase 3.62% (+0.02, +0.31; 86%; 1.30% to 4.03%). Deposits: households overnight 0.30%, agreed maturity 2.37% (€159.9 billion of new deposits); firms overnight 0.68%, agreed maturity 2.48% (€289.0 billion). The cost of borrowing by month for six months. It says what the figures are not: a description of what already happened, not a signal or a forecast.
- **Latency, found by the first whole browser run.** The card read "unavailable (the request timed out)" in that run, with the provider waiting 45 s like the other ECB cards: the browser check listed it as not checked, as designed (`liveCard`), and nothing was lost. Timed cold (the ECB caches what it has just computed, so a repeated request answers in 0.1 to 0.9 s and hides the real time) the four requests took 10 to 49 s with counts nobody had asked for, 84 s and 73 s in another round and once no answer within 120 s: far slower than the BSI cards. The provider now waits 90 s for each request (the four go out together, so the card waits at most about 90 s), the phone 100 s and the browser check 110 s; a success is kept 6 hours and a failure 2 minutes as before, so a timeout shows for two minutes and the next view asks again. `macro_refresh` has 180 s for every macro source one after the other, so one slow source can leave the ones after it for the next run, 15 minutes later (loop-rules item 19, d).
- **Found while building.** `fail_reason` clips the text of a failed data check to 110 characters, so a long series name hides the figure that failed (the first version of the units refusal for a deposit volume read "... fixed for over one yea..."): the names are now short ("household term deposits", "loans for house purchase"), and the tests build the expected visible reason with `fail_reason(ValueError(...))`. Several of my first test numbers were wrong when run (a hand sum of the house purchase average: 274,597.92, not 274,597.47; the months of the largest gaps; a message in the wrong order of checks) and were corrected from the figures, not from the module; a test of the composite gap with an `or` between two possible messages was rewritten into one exact message per case.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_eurobankrates.py`, 66 tests with a saved real response (`tests/fixtures/ecb_euro_bank_rates.json`: the four requests, 125 months of 40 series) and hand-checkable worlds in binary-exact numbers (volumes and rates chosen so that the weighted average is exact, gaps of exactly the tolerance and of exactly the limit, both signs, the oldest month of each window, the 37th month outside it); every check one at a time on a world that passes the others, and a test of their order; the largest gaps reported with sign, month and block (and the earlier of two equal gaps kept); the roundings of the printed figures with numbers whose one-, two- and three-decimal forms differ; every kind of failure of the provider. **Breakage run: 377 deliberate changes in the first table (limits and tolerances, keys and requests, the parser, every comparison and message of the checks, the ten-year range, the rows, the summary, the provider), 372 caught; the 5 that survived were 3 gaps (the source's wording, a short row without a value, the note's sentence on the provisional month: each closed with a test line) and 2 equivalent changes (`dict.get(None)` against `dict.get("")`; the sign of a gap that is only used through `abs`), left out of the table; second table 378 of 378 caught, and the whole table (379 changes) once more after the timeout was raised: 379 of 379.** Whole backend suite 2,684 passed. Phone: typecheck, 78 unit tests, `npm run export:web`; browser run 62 of 62 in the whole run (the new card read 'unavailable: the request timed out' in that run, listed as not checked, as designed); with the timeouts raised the healthy scenario ran again: 31 of 31, every live card checked; the healthy scenario checked the new card's content against the real response (every live card had data), and three screenshots (top, middle, bottom of the card) were read: the sections, the ten-year ranges, the deposits and the six monthly rows are legible at phone width, no raw ISO dates.
- **Not verified.** That the ECB's published rates are the rates that banks really charged (they are the reporting banks' own statistics, averaged); the ECB's terms of re-use (from memory); the composites' moving-average weights (not published in the series, so the composite is bounded, not rebuilt); the card on a real phone and with a real screen reader. The check text and the note still say their checks twice (backlog in `LOOP.md`).

- **The gap.** The 10 UTC audit says that the paper record is what its own rules say it is, but nothing reads the rules themselves back. The daily report's `frozen_check` is `Registry.check_frozen()`, which looks only at the registry's challengers and champions; there are none (the dashboard shows `challengers: []`, `champions: []`, `frozen_check: []`), so that check has had nothing to check so far. And the daily run trades every account from `meta["family"]` and `meta["params"]` (`PaperBook.catch_up`), not from the specification stored beside them or from its fingerprint: a hand edit of an account's parameters, or a stored specification that no longer hashes to its fingerprint, would have passed every check we have. - **The rules added to `audit.py`** (still read-only, still nothing from the engine; the fingerprint is written out again there: the first 16 hexadecimal characters of the SHA-256 of the specification as JSON with sorted keys). For every account: its meta carries a specification and a fingerprint; the specification hashes to the fingerprint; the ledger's opening row has the same fingerprint, family, parameters and stage as the meta; the family and parameters that trade are those of the stored specification; the specification charges the fee and slippage the replay checks (0.10% and 0.05%); an account is one of the three fixed names with its stage (`buy_hold` and `cash` benchmarks, `portfolio`) or else a monitor, a challenger or a champion whose id is its family and the first 8 characters of its fingerprint (so a monitor that calls itself a benchmark is refused); and a registered account's specification is in `registry/candidates.jsonl` with the same rules, logged no later than the account opened (judged by the first record when a run was repeated). A registry that cannot be read is one problem, said once, and the accounts are audited all the same; the registry's own records are not audited.

## 2026-11-18 15:27 UTC (Claude, data job): euro-area employment and productivity (people in work, hours, activities and output per worker; ECB Data Portal, MNA dataflow), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Source and why this one.** The 11 UTC note named three candidates and all three were listed live. The euro-area sovereign-stress series (CISS dataflow, 11 countries and two euro-area aggregates, 7,010 weekdays since 2000-01-05, none missing) were analysed first and passed over: the two aggregates are never above the highest country but lie BELOW the lowest country on 1,012 (equal weights) and 820 (GDP weights) days, so no "between the parts" property holds and only an upper bound is left (the CISS method weights by the cross-country correlation, not by an average). Employment and productivity (MNA) had real checks: two breakdowns that Eurostat compiles separately and that add up to the total. It also completes the GDP card of the same dataflow: the output and the people who produce it.
- **Listing first, then the whole record, then the limits.** `.../MNA/Q.Y.I10` followed by eleven dots listed all 280 seasonally adjusted series of the euro area in 7 s (a shorter key answers HTTP 400; the key has 14 dimensions). Employment `EMP` has 23 series (persons and hours: the total and ten activities), `SAL` and `SELF` the employees and self-employed, `LPR_PS` and `LPR_HW` productivity, `ULC_PS` and `COM_*` labour costs. Metadata first: every employment series is compiled by Eurostat (4D0), persons and hours in thousands (`UNIT_MULT` 3), status A, no observation comment; productivity growth is the ECB's calculation (4F0, `PCT` and `YTYPCT` of a level), so it cannot check its inputs. The whole record: 26 employment series with 126 quarters from 1995-Q1 (total persons 127), none missing. What it showed: (1) the ten activities add up to the total and employees plus the self-employed add up to the total, persons and hours, to floating-point noise: at most 5.8e-10 and 8.1e-10 thousand persons, 1.3e-7 and 1.0e-7 thousand hours in all 126 quarters (so the checks reach back over the whole window: rule 17); (2) productivity growth equals real GDP growth over employment (or hours) growth to 4.4e-14 points in 126, 125, 123 and 122 quarters, so the ECB divides real GDP (not value added) by persons or hours and the check proves units and alignment only (rule 14); (3) the extremes that set the loose limits (see the DATA_SOURCES row). The limits were written down before any test (they are in the module's comments with the real extremes).
- **What the card shows** (`GET /api/macro/euro-employment`, `atlas_api/euroemployment.py`; Macro tab right after the euro-area GDP card, `EuroEmploymentCard.tsx`): 176.5 million people in work in 2026-Q3 (+0.01% on the quarter, +0.35% on a year earlier) and the last six quarters; for the newest quarter with a breakdown (2026-Q2): 68.7 billion hours worked, 389.0 hours a person in the quarter (+0.60% a year), employees 85.9% of the people in work and 82.1% of the hours, the self-employed 14.1% and 17.9%; the ten activities by size with persons, share and change on a year (public services, education and health 44.4 million, 25.1% of jobs, +507 thousand; industry -107 thousand; information and communication -103 thousand; agriculture and fishing -101 thousand); real GDP per person in work +0.41% on the quarter and +0.95% on the year (2026-Q3), per hour worked +0.52% and +0.59% (2026-Q2). The card says what each check is: plain checks, two identities that are Eurostat's own arithmetic (largest gaps under 0.000001) and one that is true by construction.
- **Latency.** Cold, the four requests took 15.1, 12.8, 30.2 and 23.7 s; a fifth (unit labour costs) timed out at 121 s once and took 35 s the next time, which is why unit labour costs were left out. The provider waits 90 s for each request, the phone 100 s, with the quick retry of the bank rates card when it was unavailable (lesson 19d).
- **Found while building.** The first run of the tests on the real response refused it ("real GDP is missing for 2015-Q2"): total employment is a quarter ahead of its breakdown and productivity per hour a quarter behind productivity per person, and every request asks for 45 quarters, so the oldest quarter of one series has no partner in the other (persons: 44 of 45 quarters can be compared with the breakdown; the oldest quarter of productivity per hour has no real GDP). Now only the quarters both have are compared, how many is reported (`persons_quarters_checked`), a quarter older than real GDP's window is skipped and a quarter missing inside it is refused. A level of 99,999.5 printed as "100,000" in a message about the limit 100,000 (round half to even in `:,.0f`): levels show one decimal. The first wording of the productivity refusal ran to 120 characters and `fail_reason` would have clipped the figure: shortened to 93; the two refusals for the status sums ran to 112 characters and were shortened (no "the", no "total", "hours" for "hours worked"), and a test now builds every kind of refusal with large figures and wants at most 110 characters. Two percent limits cannot be hit exactly in floating point (10% a quarter, shares): probed 0.01 either side and the `<=`/`<` at the exact edge listed below as equivalent.
- **Verified.** Tests first: `tests/test_euroemployment.py`, 69 tests with a saved real response (`tests/fixtures/ecb_euro_employment.json`: the four requests, 45 quarters of 31 series, fetched 14:33 UTC) and hand-checkable worlds in binary-exact numbers (sector persons that are whole thousands, employees three quarters of the total, 388 hours a person, a real GDP level that rises by exactly 1.0625 so that productivity is exactly 6.25), the sums, the productivity relation and each range broken on its own at each side of its limit, every series in every unit it should not report, 155 missing quarters (every series, five positions), and the class (four requests, cache, failure memory, demo, a client made here, every answer but 200, the route), and one that builds every kind of refusal and wants at most 110 characters of each. `tests/test_macro_notes.py` now covers the new note (at most 1,100 characters, one plain-checks sentence, the Check line on the card). **Breakage run: first table 438 changes, 402 caught and 36 not: 8 equivalent (a string that is already a string, two ways of printing a limit that never differ, `<=` against `<` at exactly 10% in a quarter, which floating point cannot land on, and three comparisons of a quarter with real GDP's window that its own hole check always pre-empts; tried apart, they went unnoticed and are out of the table) and 28 gaps in the tests, closed by 16 tests (the source text and the request ids pinned; a row of the CSV cut short; every activity and the self-employed at the exact edge of their share range and just beyond it; the oldest quarter of the breakdown and of the move of employment; the larger of the two sums' gaps; the headline and the breakdown read at their own quarters; the decimals of hours; productivity per hour apart from productivity per person; a client made here, 90 s and closed; the date of a cached answer; any answer but 200); second table 430 changes, 430 caught, the file restored byte for byte.** Whole backend suite 2,830 passed; phone typecheck and 78 unit tests; `npm run export:web`; browser run 62 of 62 in the whole run, run twice (the first time four FRED cards, agripos, fincond, housing and industrial, were unavailable and listed as not checked, as designed; the second time every live card had data, so every content check of the new card ran too); the three screenshots of the card were read (top, the ten activities, bottom).

## 2026-11-18 18:12 UTC (Claude, data job): US job openings and labour turnover (BLS JOLTS with payroll employment and unemployed persons, via FRED), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Source and why this one.** The 14 UTC note named a US source last and said JOLTS had a separations identity to test. It was fetched live this cycle (all 14 series HTTP 200, 0.7 to 1.1 s, 1.2 s for the 14 at once) and the series pages were read: Source U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, seasonally adjusted, "Public Domain: Citation Requested", no third-party notice and no "calculated by" note (the reason the housing starts card left one series out). It is the first US labour-market card beyond weekly jobless claims, and it has two relations that can fail.
- **Whole record first.** 310 months (2000-12 to 2026-09) for each of the 12 JOLTS series, on the same months and none skipped; payrolls on all of them; unemployed persons missing 2025-10 (no household survey during the shutdown). (1) Total separations minus quits, layoffs and other: 0 in 210 months, +1 in 51, -1 in 49, so the arithmetic bound (four values each rounded to a thousand) is 2.0 and the real largest gap 1.0. (2) Each published rate against its level: over payroll employment for five of them and over payrolls PLUS openings for the openings rate; the largest gap over 1,860 comparisons is 0.0504 (4 above 0.05, none above 0.0505), the arithmetic bound 0.051; payrolls alone as the openings denominator would be wrong by up to 0.63. Tolerance 2 to the power -4 (0.0625, exact in binary). (3) Hires minus separations is NOT the change in payrolls (median gap 33 thousand, largest 12,967 thousand): not checked, said so. (4) A month's move cannot be bounded (layoffs +564% in March 2020): no move check. The limits are in the module's docstring and constants, set before any test.
- **What the card shows** (`GET /api/macro/jolts`, `atlas_api/jolts.py`; Macro tab right after the jobless claims card, `JoltsCard.tsx`): 7.523 million job openings in 2026-09 (+333 thousand on the month, +4.9% on a year), rate 4.5% (4.3% a year earlier), the 10-year range of the rate (3.4% in April 2020 to 7.5% in March 2022, 39% of the months lower), 1.06 openings per unemployed person (7.109 million unemployed; 0.94 a year earlier; range 0.20 to 2.04, 44% lower), and hires, total separations, quits, layoffs and discharges and other separations with level, rate, rate a year earlier and change on a year; the Check line says what was checked and that the two relations show the series belong together, not that the figures are right.
- **Found while building.** (a) **A combined FRED request applies one `cosd` to the first series only**: the first download of the 12 JOLTS series with `cosd=2015-01-01` had 141 months of openings and 310 of the other 11, no error and no warning (my own first comparison printed "differs in 0 months" because it compared only the months both had). Each series is asked for alone; a test fails on a comma in an id. (b) The newest JOLTS month can be a month ahead of the household survey, or behind it (it was behind by a real hole in 2025-10): the openings per unemployed person use the newest month in the last 120 that has both figures and say which month it is; a year-earlier ratio that cannot be worked out is `None`, not a guess. (c) The first draft of the note was 1,164 characters (the limit is 1,100): trimmed. (d) A test of mine built a world where a year-earlier layoffs level broke the sum; the module was right and the test wrong.
- **Verified.** Tests first: `tests/test_jolts.py`, 96 tests with a saved real response (`tests/fixtures/fred_jolts.json`: 14 series fetched 17:28 UTC, 310 months of JOLTS, 322 of payrolls and unemployed persons) and worlds in binary-exact numbers (payrolls 120,000, openings 8,000, hires 6,000, separations 4,800, quits 2,400, layoffs 1,800, other 600 and 8,000 unemployed, so every rate is exact: 6.25, 5, 4, 2, 1.5, 0.5): each range at each side of its limit for every series, the sum and each of the six rates at exactly the tolerance, a hair above and in both directions, every month looked at, the denominators, the real record (310 months, largest gaps 1.0 and 0.0504) and the 137-month download the card makes, the figures worked by hand from the files, the class (14 requests, cache, failure memory, demo, a client made here, any answer but 200, one series failing, the route); `tests/test_macro_notes.py` now covers the new note. **Breakage run: first table 376 changes, 366 caught and 10 not: 4 equivalent (a number printed with the same text two ways: `endswith("0")` against `endswith(".0")` on a string that always has one decimal, `{RATE_TOLERANCE}` against `{RATE_TOLERANCE:g}` for 0.0625, a 120-month window written as the constant or as its own length when the constant is also the minimum, and a rate's level name reached through the rate's map or directly; tried apart with `day: greater than`, they all survive, as they must) and 6 gaps in the tests, closed by 6 new or changed tests (a year-earlier ratio taken from the openings month instead of the ratio month, where both gave 0.94; levels shown rounded when they have decimals; a month's change at two decimals instead of one; the start of the ratio window when its first month has no unemployed figure; the full wording of the demo line; ## 2026-11-18 23:53 UTC (Claude, data job): US gross domestic product by component (BEA National Income and Product Accounts via FRED), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Source and terms.** The Bureau of Economic Analysis' national accounts through FRED (`fredgraph.csv`, free, no key): seven nominal levels (`GDP`, `PCEC`, `GPDI`, `GCE`, `NETEXP`, `EXPGS`, `IMPGS`), real growth (`A191RL1Q225SBEA`) and its four contributions (`DPCERY2Q224SBEA`, `A006RY2Q224SBEA`, `A822RY2Q224SBEA`, `A019RY2Q224SBEA`), twelve series, ONE request each. All twelve series pages were read 2026-11-18 23:2x UTC: Source BEA, release "Gross Domestic Product" (tables 1.1.5 and 1.1.2), "Public Domain: Citation Requested", no third-party notice; updated 2026-10-29 (the first estimate of 2026-Q3), next release 2026-11-25. Fetched live: HTTP 200 for all twelve, 1.7 to 2.3 s each. Two ids went wrong first: FRED's `PCE` is the MONTHLY series and `DPCERC1Q027SBEA` answered 404; the quarterly consumer spending is `PCEC`.
- **The whole record first, limits before any test** (decisions file in the session's scratch folder). 319 quarters from 1947-Q1 (318 of growth and contributions), none missing, all twelve ending 2026-Q3. GDP minus its four parts: 0.000 in 181 quarters, 0.001 in 137, 0.002 in one (1984-Q3); net exports minus (exports minus imports): 0.000 in 253, 0.001 in 66; real growth minus its four contributions: 0.00 to 0.07, the largest in 1950-Q2. Each gap is rounding of the published decimals (the arithmetic bounds are 0.0025, 0.0015 and 0.05 + 4 x 0.005 = 0.07), so the tolerances are 2 to the power -8, 2 to the power -9 and 9/128, all exact in binary (2 to the power -4 would have refused the real record on the contributions). Ranges set around the real ones since 2000; OVERDUE after 240 days from the 1st of the newest quarter (the next quarter's first estimate comes about 211 days after it).
- **Built.** `atlas_api/usgdp.py` (class `UsGdp`, route `/api/macro/us-gdp`, the same cache, failure memory and quick-retry as the other Macro cards, wired into `app.py`, the `macro_refresh` job and the extras), `tests/fixtures/fred_usgdp.json` (51 quarters of each series from 2014-Q1, the real responses), `tests/test_usgdp.py` (121 tests: every limit at both edges with the other identities still exact, every check in the oldest and the newest quarter, the order of the checks, the quarter arithmetic across the turn of the year, the newest quarter worked by hand from the real responses) and the phone card `UsGdpCard.tsx` (headline with the change on the quarter and the year, real growth with its comparisons and its ten-year range, the four parts with share and contribution, the last six quarters, the Check line, the note, the source link) with types, client, keys, a quick-retry query, a place after the JOLTS card, an entry in `test_macro_notes.py` and a block in the browser script.

Phone: type check clean, 79 unit tests. Browser: 62 of 62 in the whole run (every live card had data, so every content check of the new card ran too). Breakage run: first table 388 changes, 383 caught and 5 not: all five were gaps in the tests, closed by tests (the quarter label and the count back were only ever asked for the first month of a quarter, so three changes that give the same answer there survived; the lowest value of the range was never fractional; the cache was never asked at exactly the end of its time); the five equivalent changes (the date written with `str`, the pair loop written from the start, the earliest of ties written with sorted dates, an integer zero start and a timeout written as a float) tried apart: all five survive, as they must; final table 388 changes, 388 caught, the file restored byte for byte. ## 2026-11-19 00:31 UTC (Claude, improve job): the STATE BOARD chore is a tool (`atlas/devtools/board.py`) instead of a new scratch script every hour
- **Review.** Everything ran on the code of 23:53 UTC and today's data: the whole backend suite (3,199 passed in 3 min 30 s), the phone type check and 79 unit tests, and the browser suite (the whole run 62 of 62 at 23:33 UTC, the `healthy` scenario alone 31 of 31 at 00:20 UTC, after the daily run, with every live card carrying data). Screenshots read: the Journey top (the research verdict, "0 of 15 strategies passed", and the waiting decision card) and its feed (the new fill of `volume_surge-9c042d26` (its first) reads "BOUGHT 0.0126 BTC at $64,905.62; fee $0.82, slippage cost $0.41" above the BUY signal that made it, both at 00:00 UTC on 18 Nov, which is when the candle closed and the next one opened) and the new US GDP card (top and bottom). No wrong figure, unclear sentence or ugly state found in those screens; the daily run's data changed no wording.
- **Decision.** Candidates were the board chore as a tool, the breakage harness, the daily-run rehearsal tool (all three in the `LOOP.md` backlog), the Silver futures check that fails when CFTC has nothing for a contract, and the older providers' check messages that `fail_reason` clips at 110 characters. The board chore wins because it is done every single hour (it has been written as a new scratch script thirteen times in a row, and one of those scripts lost its values to a shell heredoc), it can be made safe by refusing before it writes, and its blast radius is two Markdown files that a test already guards. The harness is next.
- **Built.** `atlas/devtools/board.py` (standard library; `add` and `check`; about 160 lines): the row goes on top of the table under the line that starts with `## STATE BOARD`; the oldest rows beyond 20 go directly under the archive's table header, newest first, with one `Moved on ...` line above the earlier ones; the cells are read from files; and nothing is written unless the result has five columns in every row, the board newest first, no row both on the board and in the archive and none twice in the archive. It refuses a time that is not a stamp, an empty cell or one with a pipe or a line break, a row older than the newest or already there, a board row in the wrong form, and an archive without its header or notes, each with its reason and exit code 1; `--dry-run` writes nothing. `atlas/devtools/tests/test_board.py` has 56 tests on made-up boards (one-row and many-row moves, the other tables left alone, every refusal with its exact words, the options, the default time and label, running as a script). The header of `HANDOFF.md`, `AGENT_NOTES.md` (same bullet), the devtools README and the `LOOP.md` backlog point to it. This cycle's own row was written with it.
- **Verified.** The 56 tests; a breakage run of 151 one-line changes of the tool: 127 caught at first and 24 not (five of them equivalent: the regex anchors that `re.match` already has, the archive header's `(UTC)`, a comparison that can never be equal, and the oldest of a single moved row), 19 closed by tests (a two-digit year, a first cell that is a stamp followed by text, rows dated 2027, a board kept down to one row, a new row older than the newest but newer than the oldest, a `###` heading inside the board section, a heading that only starts like the board's, an empty board followed by a dated row, an archive that ends with its header, a line that starts with `Moved` but not `Moved on`, two moved rows, a larger `--keep`, two problems in one refusal, the default folder, `--who`, `--keep` and the word `none`); final table 146 changes, 146 caught, the file restored byte for byte, the five equivalent ones tried apart and all five survive. On the real files `board.py check` says "board and archive are fine" and a dry run names the row it would move.

- **Built.** `atlas_api/btcquant/rehearse.py` (about 175 lines; the project's own `daily`, `data`, `config` and `failreason`, otherwise the standard library) and `tests/test_btcquant_rehearse.py`. `cd atlas/backend && python3 -m atlas_api.btcquant.rehearse [--candle real|fall|rise] [--move PERCENT]` copies `atlas/btcquant` to a temporary folder, runs `daily.run_daily` there on the stored candles plus one stand-in candle at 00:15 UTC on the day after it, runs it a second time on the same input, and prints in plain lines what the run would do (status, protocol, frozen check, stale or not, every account's days and equity before and after and its share in Bitcoin, halted accounts, orders waiting for the next open, how many files changed). The stand-in is the day's own still-forming Coinbase candle (one request; `real`, the default), a candle 20% lower (`fall`) or 20% higher (`rise`), or any move in percent; the synthetic ones open at the previous close and put the high and low half a percent outside the open and close. Findings, each in words: the run or its repeat raised an error; the frozen check is not empty; the data would be stale; a stored account is missing afterwards or did not step by a day (an account that caught up several days is a note); the repeat changed a state file, a ledger or a report, or changed the dashboard in anything but its run-activity counters (`processed` per account, `events` per step); or any file in the repository's `btcquant` folder differs after the rehearsal (every file is hashed before and after; the tool never writes there). Exit code 0, 1 (a finding) or 2 (it could not be made, for example Coinbase has no candle yet for the new day: it says so and names the other kinds). The daily run does not import it. It replaces a scratch script that was written by hand four times (2026-11-17 16:24 UTC and 2026-11-18 04:19, 19:21 and 22:23 UTC); `btcquant/DAILY_LOOP.md` explains its use and `LOOP.md` marks the backlog item done.

One survivor is equivalent: the tool demands exactly one candle of the day, so its first and last row are the same row.

## 2026-11-19 03:02 UTC (Claude, data job): US household income, spending and saving (BEA Personal Income and Outlays and the GDP release via FRED), a new card on the Signal Macro tab
- **Source and terms.** The Bureau of Economic Analysis' monthly Personal Income and Outlays and the GDP release's consumer spending by type, through FRED (`fredgraph.csv`, free, no key): eight monthly series (`PI`, `W055RC1`, `DSPI`, `A068RC1`, `PCE`, `B069RC1`, `PMSAVE`, and the rate `PSAVERT`) and four quarterly ones (`PCEC`, `PCDG`, `PCND`, `PCESV`), twelve in all, ONE request each, every request from 1959-01-01 so that the whole record is checked each time. The eleven new series pages were read 2026-11-19 02:2x UTC (`PCEC` on 2026-11-18): Source BEA, "Public Domain: Citation Requested", no third-party notice; updated 2026-10-29, next release 2026-11-25. The note of `PSAVERT` says personal saving is income less outlays and taxes and the rate is saving over disposable income: BEA derives them, so the sums below prove alignment, not accuracy (said on the card). Fetched live: HTTP 200 for all twelve, 0.4 to 1.1 s each. **Ids from memory were wrong** (`DDURRC1Q027SBEA`, `DNDGRC1Q027SBEA`, `DSERRC1Q027SBEA`: HTTP 404; they are the BEA account codes printed on the series pages, the FRED ids are `PCDG`, `PCND`, `PCESV`, quarterly despite their names; `A067RC1`, `A071RC1`, `B070RC1`, `A074RC1` answered 404 too, and `DSPI`, `PMSAVE` and `W055RC1` are the ids that exist).
- **The whole record first, limits before any test** (decisions file in the session's scratch folder). 813 months from 1959-01 to 2026-09 for the eight monthly series (none missing, the same months in all) and 271 quarters from 1959-Q1 for the four quarterly ones. Personal income minus taxes minus disposable income: 0.0 in 610 months and 0.1 in 203; disposable income minus outlays minus saving: 0.0 in 596 and 0.1 in 217 (three values each rounded to a tenth: bound 0.15, tolerance 5/32); the saving rate against 100 x saving / disposable income: largest gap 0.0548 (1968-07), arithmetic bound 0.05 for the decimal plus the rounding of the levels, 0.0658 in 1959-01 when disposable income was 351.5 and 0.0507 since 2000, so the tolerance is 9/128, not 2 to the power -4; the three types against the quarterly total: 0.000 in 195 quarters, 0.001 in 124 (tolerance 2 to the power -8); **the quarterly consumer spending against the average of the monthly consumer spending of its three months, two tables of the same Bureau: largest gap 0.0457 in 271 quarters against a rounding bound of 0.0505 (tolerance 2 to the power -4)**; outlays minus consumer spending minus interest (what is left): 0.7 to 307.2 in all 813 months, never negative (a property, floor -5/32). Ranges set around the real ones since 1959 (saving rate 1.4 in 2005-07 to 31.8 in 2020-04). The newest month is OVERDUE after 100 days from its 1st (a month is released 55 to 61 days after its 1st and stays the newest until the next release; September 79 days old on 2026-11-19, 85 on the day before October's release).
- **What the card shows** (`GET /api/macro/us-household`, `atlas_api/ushousehold.py`; Macro tab right after the US GDP card, `UsHouseholdCard.tsx`): households saved 3.8% of their after-tax income in September 2026, down from 4.0% the month before and 5.1% a year earlier, and 7% of the 813 months since 1959 had a lower rate (lowest 1.4% in July 2005, highest 31.8% in April 2020); personal income 27.708 trillion dollars at an annual rate (+0.20% on the month, +4.15% on the year), taxes 3.419 trillion, disposable income 24.289 trillion (+0.22%, +4.64%), outlays 23.378 trillion (+0.49%, +6.15%) of which consumer spending 22.453 trillion (96.0%), interest payments 617.9 billion (2.6%) and everything else 307.2 billion (1.3%, by subtraction), saving 911.1 billion (down 60.3 billion on the month and 278.8 billion on the year: spending is growing faster than income); the quarterly split of consumer spending (Q3 2026, 22.313 trillion): services 69.0%, nondurable goods 20.2%, durable goods 10.8%, each up about 6% on the year; the last six months; the Check line and the note, which say what the checks do and do not prove.
- **Built.** `atlas_api/ushousehold.py` (class `UsHousehold`, the same cache, failure memory and quick-retry as the other Macro cards, wired into `app.py`, the `macro_refresh` job and the extras), `tests/fixtures/fred_ushousehold.json` (180 months from 2011-10 and 60 quarters of each series, the real responses), `tests/test_ushousehold.py` (178 tests: all 48 range edges in one table with what speaks next when a value sits inside an edge, every sum at its tolerance and just over it in the oldest, the newest and a middle period, the order of the checks, the cross-table comparison for each month of a quarter and its neighbours, the skipped quarter newer than the months, the rounding of every figure, the newest month worked by hand from the real responses), the phone card with types, client, keys, a quick-retry query, a place after the GDP card, an entry in `test_macro_notes.py` and a block in the browser script (with a middle screenshot), loop-rules item 24 and a hint in `DATA_LOOP.md`.
- **Found while building.** (a) The first smoke run on the real data marked the card OVERDUE: my threshold (75 days) forgot that a month stays the newest until the NEXT release, so the age to allow is the release lag plus a month (about 85 to 91 days), now 100. (b) The ids from memory: see above. (c) Interlocking limits cannot all be reached by a consistent world (personal income cannot sit at its lower limit while disposable income and taxes are inside theirs), so each edge test says which check must speak next instead (income 200.0 makes the income sum speak, 199.9 the range); one limit (interest at 2.0) lets everything pass. (d) A test pinned a whole number with a decimal (277,078.0) where the shared helper writes 277,078: the test, not the module, was wrong.
- **Verified.** Backend: the new tests, the whole backend suite 3,436 passed in 4 min 4 s (the 3,253 of 01:39 UTC plus the 183 new tests). Phone: type check clean, 79 unit tests. Browser: the whole run 62 of 62 (every live card had data, so every content check of the new card ran too) (the `healthy` scenario alone 31 of 31 on the first run, every live card had data so every content check of the new card ran; screenshots read: the top, the middle and the bottom of the card, no defect). (c) 191 tests passed on the first run, which proves little (loop-rules item 18b): the first breakage run (357 one-line changes, 349 caught) found five test gaps and three equivalent changes. The gaps: a skip of a month right after the oldest month, or before the newest, went unseen (`zip(months[1:], months[2:])` leaves the first pair out); the closest margin and the smallest rest are reported to three decimals and no test had a value whose two- and three-decimal forms differ (2.125 and 799.875 now); and the order of equal sizes was tested only where the table order, the labels and the keys all agree, so sorting on the keys instead of the labels survived (two ties where they disagree close it). Three tests closed the five gaps. (d) The note is 1,099 of the 1,100 characters the shared note test allows.
- **Verified.** Backend: 194 tests in `tests/test_joltsindustries.py` and 5 more in `tests/test_macro_notes.py` from the new entry. Breakage run of the provider: 357 one-line changes, 349 caught the first time and 8 survived (3 equivalent, 5 test gaps, closed by the 3 added tests); the final table of 354 changes (the equivalents taken out) was caught 354 of 354 in 556 s, the file restored and its hash checked. The equivalent changes (5 entries of 4 kinds: a copy of the dictionary, the months taken from private instead of the total, the total compared with itself, listed twice, and `!=` against `>` on a count of distinct dates) were run on their own again on 2026-11-24: 5 of 5 survive, 0 killed, as they must. Whole backend suite: 3,632 passed in 4 min 2 s on 2026-11-19 (before the last three tests) and 3,635 passed in 3 min 44 s on 2026-11-24 with them. Phone: type check clean, 79 unit tests. Browser (2026-11-19): the `healthy` scenario 31 of 31 and the whole run 62 of 62, every live card had data so every content check of the new card ran too (35 lines of checks of its text); the screenshots read (top, middle and bottom of the card): the rows wrap cleanly, the parts sit under their group in small type, the Check line and the note at the bottom read plainly, no defect found.

It was, after this entry was written: the `healthy` scenario passed 30 of 31, the Macro test stopping at the DEALERS card because the New York Fed had revised its own figures (the last entry of this log, on the dealers card, tells the cause), not at this card; the same scenario in a scratch copy that left the dealers check out passed 31 of 31 with every JOLTS check, and its screenshots of this card on live data were read: rows wrap cleanly, the parts sit under their group, no defect.)
